--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page begins the preface "To the Reader" for Robert Boyle's "New Experiments Physico-Mechanical," following the title page of the "Second Part" or "Examination." It addresses the transition from a private correspondence to a published scientific work.</meta>

<image-desc>Two horizontal rows of repeated fleur-de-lis style ornaments forming a decorative header at the top of the page.</image-desc>

## To the Reader.

<image-desc>Large ornamental drop cap letter 'A' featuring intricate floral and vine-like motifs within a square frame.</image-desc>

*Although the following treatise is far more lengthy <note>original: "prolix"</note> than is appropriate for a letter, and longer than I at first intended it to be, I am very unwilling to increase the already excessive size of the book with a preface. Yet, there are some particulars that I think myself obliged to bring to the reader’s attention, as things that it will either be in his interest to know, or in mine to have known.*

*In the first place, then: if it is asked why I publish a letter to the world—which, by its style and various passages, appears to have been written for a specific person as much as addressed to them—I have two main reasons to give. The first is that many of the experiments related within it were tried in the presence of clever <term>ingenious men</term> <note>In the 17th century, "ingenious" referred to people of high intelligence, scientific curiosity, or technical skill.</note>, and because those experiments have made—*

<summary>In this preface, Robert Boyle explains why he is publishing a scientific work originally written as a private letter and apologizes for its length. He justifies the publication by noting that the experiments were witnessed by other respected intellectuals.</summary>
<keywords>Robert Boyle, Preface, Scientific Method, Experiments, Correspondence, Ingenious Men, 17th-century literature</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface "To the Reader" from Robert Boyle’s "New Experiments Physico-Mechanical." Boyle is explaining the reasons for publishing his scientific findings, citing high demand for copies and the potential benefit to the scientific community.</meta>

### To the Reader

*...made some noise among the* **Virtuosi** <note>original: "Virtuosi"; in the 17th century, this term referred to scholars, scientists, or people with a deep interest in the arts and sciences.</note> (*so much so that some of them have been sent to* **foreign countries**, *where they have had the luck not to be despised), I could not—without completely tiring more than one* <term>amanuensis</term> <note>A scribe or assistant who copies documents by hand.</note>—*give out half as many copies of them as were so earnestly desired that I could not civilly refuse them. The other reason is that intelligent* **persons** *in matters of this kind persuaded me that the publication of what I had observed regarding the* **nature** *of the* **air** *would not be useless to the* **world**; *and that in an* **age** *as fascinated by* **novelties** *as ours is, these new* **experiments** *would be welcome to the* **lovers** *of free and real* **learning**. *Thus, I might at once fulfill my grand* **design** *of promoting* **experimental** *and* **useful philosophy** <note>In this period, "philosophy" referred to the study of the natural world, or what we now call science.</note>, *and obtain the great satisfaction of providing some to ingenious* **men**; *the hope of which is, I confess, a temptation that I cannot easily resist.*

*Regarding my being somewhat lengthy* <note>original: "prolix"</note> *in many of my* **experiments**, *I have these* **reasons** *to offer: First, that some of them being altogether new, they seemed to need a detailed description* <note>original: "being circumstantially related"</note> *to keep the* **reader** *from doubting them. Second, there were various* **circumstances** *that I recorded here and there for fear of forgetting them, when I might later...*

<summary>Robert Boyle explains that he is publishing his work to meet the high demand for copies among scholars and to promote the advancement of experimental science. He also justifies the length of his descriptions as necessary for establishing the credibility of these new experiments.</summary>
<keywords>Robert Boyle, Virtuosi, Scientific Communication, Air, Experimental Philosophy, 17th-century Science, Scribes</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues Robert Boyle's preface "To the Reader." Boyle is explaining why his descriptions of experiments are so detailed and lengthy, citing the difficulty and expense of the equipment as reasons why most readers will not be able to replicate them personally.</meta>

# To the Reader.

->A 4<-

*...have occasion to make use of them in my other writings. In several cases, I thought it necessary to describe things in great detail* <note>original: "circumstantially"</note> *so that the person I addressed them to might be able to repeat such unusual experiments without mistake and with as little trouble as possible. After I consented to let my observations be made public, the most common reason for my wordiness* <note>original: "prolixity"</note> *was that I foresaw the trouble I met with in conducting these trials carefully. The great expense of time that they necessarily require—not to mention the cost of building the* **engine** <term>engine</term> <note>Referring to the specialized air pump Boyle and Robert Hooke designed.</note> *and employing a man to manage it—will probably discourage most men from trying these experiments again. Therefore, I thought I might do the majority of my readers a useful service by reporting what I carefully observed so precisely. They may look upon these narratives as permanent records in our new* **pneumatics** <note>original: "Pneumaticks"; the branch of physics dealing with the mechanical properties of air and other gases.</note> *and will not need to repeat the experiments themselves to have a clear enough idea of them to ground their reflections and theories upon.*

*And because sometimes it is the discussion written about the experiment that makes it appear lengthy, I have usually left a con-*

<meta>catchword: spicuous</meta>

<summary>Boyle explains that he provides detailed experimental accounts because the air pump is too expensive and difficult for most people to operate, making his written records essential for scientific study. He intends for his "narratives" to serve as a foundational record for the new field of pneumatics.</summary>

<keywords>Robert Boyle, air pump, pneumatics, experimental method, scientific records, scientific instruments, wordiness</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Robert Boyle’s preface "To the Reader." He explains the structural separation of his experimental data from his theoretical reflections and defends his cautious, non-dogmatic tone by referencing Aristotle.</meta>

# To the Reader.

...spicuous gap between such *discussions* and the *experiments* to which they belong or are attached; so that those who only want the *historical* part of the account we give of our **engine** <term>engine</term> <note>Referring to the specialized air pump Boyle and Robert Hooke designed.</note> may read the *narratives* without being troubled to read the *reflections* as well. I mention this for the sake of those who are well-versed in the **new philosophy** <note>original: "New Philosophy"; a 17th-century term for the experimental and mechanical science championed by figures like Francis Bacon and the Royal Society.</note> and in *mathematics*; so that they may skip what was intended only for people who are less acquainted with these matters than even I am (matters hardly mentioned by any writer in our language), and not for those from whom I would be much more eager to learn than to pretend to teach.

Regarding my habit of speaking rather doubtfully or hesitantly, rather than decisively, about matters where I see some difficulty, I have given a detailed and, I hope, satisfactory explanation in another *treatise* (which may, with *God’s assistance*, be published soon). Therefore, I will now defend my practice only by citing the observation of **Aristotle** <note>The ancient Greek philosopher whose authority was being challenged by the "New Philosophy."</note>, who notes somewhere *that to seem to know all things with certainty, and to speak positively about them, is a trick of bold and young men; whereas those who are in-*

<summary>Boyle explains that he separates experimental data from theory so experts can skip the basics, and he defends his cautious, non-dogmatic writing style as a mark of intellectual maturity.</summary>
<keywords>Robert Boyle, air pump, experimental method, scientific communication, Aristotle, New Philosophy, skepticism, empiricism</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s preface "To the Reader." The text begins mid-sentence due to a cropped margin, continuing a discussion on the importance of cautious language in scientific writing. The catchword "And" indicates the next page begins with that word.</meta>

->### To the Reader<-

...indeed intelligent and thoughtful, are accustomed to employ more cautious and hesitant expressions, or (as he says) "they always add a 'perhaps' and a 'maybe.'" <note>original Greek: προστιθέασιν ἀεὶ τὸ ἴσως, καὶ τὸ τάχα (prostithéasin aeí to ísos, kai to tácha)</note>

There are various reflections and other passages in the following letter, and even some experiments mentioned occasionally, which may seem either irrelevant or unnecessary, but they are not. They were written intentionally, either to prove a truth that has been challenged or to disprove some mistaken notion held by some distinguished new philosopher <term>a natural scientist of the 17th-century Scientific Revolution</term> or by other clever men. I assumed they would easily forgive me for purposefully omitting their names in such instances; however, an inquisitive reader will probably identify several of them by the opinions I disprove in the experiments I am discussing.

Ever since I recognized the usefulness of theoretical geometry to natural philosophy <term>the study of nature and the physical universe; the precursor to modern science</term>, the unfortunate ailments <note>original: "Distempers"</note> of my eyes have kept me from becoming very familiar with it. Because of this, I fear I will need the forgiveness of my mathematical readers for some passages which I would have treated more accurately had I been deeply skilled in geometry.

<summary>The author explains his use of cautious language in scientific reporting and justifies his decision to anonymously critique other philosophers. He also apologizes for any lack of mathematical precision, citing a chronic eye condition that hindered his study of geometry.</summary>

<keywords>Scientific Revolution, Natural Philosophy, Geometry, scientific methodology, academic etiquette, skepticism, Robert Boyle</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's preface "To the Reader." The previous page ended with an apology for potential mathematical inaccuracies due to the author's poor eyesight. ## To the Reader

And indeed, because of reasons I have explained elsewhere, I have deliberately kept myself a stranger to most of the new *hypotheses* <term>scientific theories or explanations</term> in *philosophy* <term>natural science</term>. I am well aware that the *engine* <term>the air pump or pneumatic engine</term> I am discussing has led me to write about some subjects that are quite far removed from those I have been most familiar with. 

Furthermore, because I was forced to write most of the following letter while away from both my *library* and my own *manuscripts*, I fear that my *discourses* <note>original: "Discourses"; refers here to his written arguments or essays</note> not only lack many excellent insights that the *learned writings* of others might have provided to enrich or improve them, but also—partly for this *reason* and partly for the one mentioned shortly before—it is possible I may have mentioned some *ideas* already published by others without acknowledging the *authors*. This was not done with any intent to rob deserving men of their credit, but because I was unaware that these specific points had already been published by them. This is especially likely since the *experiments* performed with our *engine* are themselves enough to suggest the very *ideas* we base our work upon.

The *reader* may change the order of the *experiments* as best suits their own *purpose* in reading them. For not only all

<summary>The author admits he may have inadvertently repeated ideas already published by others because he wrote this work while away from his library and personal notes. He also notes that the air pump experiments naturally suggest certain scientific theories to anyone observing them.</summary>
<keywords>scientific method, air pump, intellectual honesty, academic citations, natural philosophy, experimental observation</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s preface "To the Reader." The previous page explained that readers may reorder the experiments as they see fit, as they were not written in a strict logical or chronological sequence.</meta>

...those between which there is a natural *affinity* <term>a natural connection or relationship</term> (by belonging to one subject) are *not* always placed next to each other. Furthermore, they are not even set down in the order in which they were performed, but most commonly in the haphazard order in which my circumstances forced me to send them to the *press* <term>the printing house</term>. And, what is worse, I usually sent the earlier *experiments* away completely before the later ones were written, or perhaps even conducted. Because of this, I lost the opportunity to correct or fill in the imperfections of what I had previously written using the insights gained from my subsequent *trials* <note>original: "Tryals"; scientific tests or experiments</note> and *discoveries*.

Besides all this, an ailment in my eyes prevented me from not only writing a single *experiment* myself, but even from reading over what I had dictated to others. I cannot help but fear that, besides the *author's* own mistakes, this *edition* may be marred by many errors that should properly be blamed on a very unskilled *writer* <term>a scribe or secretary</term> (whom I was often forced by haste to employ, contrary to my usual habit). These errors may also have escaped the diligent care of that *learned friend* who does me the favor of overseeing the *press*; this is especially likely since there is a distance of a two-day journey between the printing house and myself.

I perhaps do not need to point out to the fair-minded <note>original: "equitable"</note>—

<summary>Boyle apologizes for the lack of organization in his report, explaining that his experiments were sent to the printer as they were finished rather than in a logical order. He also notes that his poor eyesight forced him to rely on unskilled scribes, likely leading to further errors in the text.</summary>
<keywords>scientific method, Robert Boyle, publishing history, eye ailment, scribes, air pump, experimental order</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s preface "To the Reader." The previous page ended mid-word with "fair-minded," which the original text rendered as "equi-table." This page begins with the second half of that word.</meta>

equitable Reader, how much the strange *confusions* of this unhappy *nation* <note>Referring to the political instability in England during the mid-17th century</note> (in the midst of which I have conducted and written these *experiments*) are likely to disturb that *calmness* of *mind* and *undistractedness* of *thoughts* that are usually necessary for *happy speculations* <term>successful scientific reflection or theoretical study</term>. But I assume that, considering all these factors together, the reader will readily perceive that I have been far from following the *poet's* <note>Referring to the Roman poet Horace</note> prudent *advice* regarding the slow *publication* of *books* intended to earn a reputation:

->—Let it be kept back until the ninth year <note>original: "Nonumque prematur in Annum"; a famous rule from Horace’s *Ars Poetica* advising authors to wait nine years before publishing to ensure their work is polished</note><-

Instead, I am allowing this *treatise* to go out into the *world* with a multitude of disadvantages.

But if it is asked why I did not make the work more suitable for the *press* before I sent it there, my *answer* must be this: at first, I did not imagine that this sort of *experiment* would prove to be nearly as troublesome to conduct or to record as I later found them to be. To encourage the *printer* to work quickly, I promised him that I would send the entire *letter* <note>original: "Epistle"</note> in a very short time. Consequently, even though the *press* occasionally stopped working because of *festivals*—

<meta>The text cuts off here as the word "festivals" is split across the page break.</meta>

<summary>Boyle explains that the political turmoil in England and his own underestimation of the difficulty of his experiments caused him to rush the publication of his work, ignoring traditional advice to delay publication for the sake of quality.</summary>
<keywords>Robert Boyle, scientific writing, publishing history, Horace, English Civil War, experimental philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous page, completing the word "festivals" (split as "festi-vals" across the break).</meta>

<header>To the Reader.</header>

*festivals, or the absence of the* proofreader <note>original: "Corrector"</note>, *gave me the leisure to write at length upon some subjects; yet being often called upon to send the papers to the* press, *my promise and many unexpected distractions <term>original: "Avocations"; here meaning minor duties or diversions that pull one away from primary work</term> obliged me to work with a haste that—though it has taken nothing away from the accuracy of the* factual <note>original: "Historical"</note> *part of our* book—*has, I fear, been disadvantageous enough to all the rest.*

*I had the less hesitation <note>original: "scruple"</note> to let the following papers pass out of my hands with all their imperfections because, given the state of public affairs and my own circumstances at the time, I did not know when (if ever) I would again be in a condition to conduct experiments of this kind. This is especially true since (aside from being almost weary of being, as it were, confined to one sort of experiment) I am already committed—if it please God to grant me life and health—to use my first leisure time for the publication of some other* scientific papers <note>original: "Physiological Papers"; in the 17th century, "physiology" referred to the study of natural philosophy and the physical world in general</note>. *I thought it would make me much better prepared to take those in hand if I first finished all that I had to write at this time concerning our* air pump <note>original: "Engine"</note>.

*I have this further to add, by way of excuse: that as it has been my design in publishing these experiments to satisfy*

<header>To the Reader.</header>

Ingenious men; so, if I have not been much flattered, I may hope that the various hints to be found in the following *Letter* will (at least) somewhat awaken men's thoughts and excite them to new speculations (such as perhaps even inquisitive men would otherwise scarcely encounter). I need not despair that the examination of such new hypotheses <note>original: "Suspicions"</note> and inquiries will, at least *occasionally*, be made easier from this point forward. I said *occasionally* because, as the proverb says, *it is easy to add to things already invented* <note>original: "Facile Inventis addere"</note>.

It seems reasonable to expect that our *machine itself* <note>original: "Engine"; referring to Boyle's air pump</note> and various of our *experiments* will be greatly improved by the industry of *inventive* and *mathematical minds*, whose designs may easily either correct or supplement—and consequently surpass—many of those we have used. Specifically, if men through skill and patience can succeed in both emptying such *glass vessels* <note>original: "Receivers"</note> as ours until there is no more *air* left in them than appeared to remain in the glasses used in the *Magdeburg experiment* <note>A famous 1654 experiment by Otto von Guericke demonstrating the power of atmospheric pressure and the vacuum</note> (which will be mentioned later), and in keeping out the *air* for a sufficient amount of time, the usefulness and discoveries of our *machine* will be significantly advanced. And perhaps that may apply to it, which I re-

### To the Reader.

<unclear>I re</unclear>member Seneca says of Nature, "We believe ourselves to be initiated, yet we are still lingering in her entrance" <note>original: "*Initiatos nos credimus, in Vestibulo ejus hæremus*"</note>. Since I am now in a place where I have access to moderately skilled craftsmen <note>original: "Artificers"</note>, I have—since finishing the following *letter*—made some *additions* to my *machine* <note>original: "Engine"; referring to Robert Boyle’s famous air pump</note. Through these improvements, I have found in some new trials that I can, without much new trouble, keep the surrounding *air* out of the emptied *glass container* <note>original: "exhausted Receiver"</note> for an entire day. I might even be able to keep it out much longer if I had the leisure to continue these *experiments*. However, I must first handle some urgent *business* and publish several *papers* that I have promised to the *press*. Such experiments might eventually provide a *supplement* to the following *treatise*, from which I *shall now no longer keep the reader.*

# To the Reader.

Several small tracts <note>original: "Tracts"; refers to short treatises or pamphlets</note> of history having been written at the end of the *History of the Cardinals*, and since that time several other matters of importance having happened, it was thought necessary—as the most appropriate place—to add this supplement to them in this volume. In this work, following the deaths of many of those individuals and the many changes which the Court of Rome is likely to undergo under a new Pope, we aim to provide further detail.

Because various interests and allegiances <note>original: "Dependencies"</note> may help us maintain our knowledge of certain things that have happened since those great affairs were published to the world—serving as a kind of memoir for those who record <note>original: "Declarators"</note> such things—we can do no better than to state that our experience may possibly afford a supplement to the following examples. From these, I shall embark on a longer journey, and then...

# To the Reader.

I do not make excuses for the slowness of the publication. I hope that the long anticipation <note>original: "expectation"</note> you have had for it will enhance, and not diminish, your delight in the enjoyment of a work <note>original: "piece"</note> that is likely to be so widely accepted among students of precise <term>Philosophy</term> <note>original: "accurate Philosophy"; in this period, "philosophy" usually referred to "natural philosophy," what we now call science.</note>.

*Bubble of Glass*) sets down his thoughts concerning the strange excess of strength in *Air*, agitated by heat, beyond what the same has when unagitated, 283 etc. And then proceeds to the examination of the weight of the *Air* by an *Aeolipile*, and compares the result of it with that of *Mersennus*, 286. The *Opinions* and *Experiments* of various *Authors*, and some of his own, regarding the proportion of weight between *Water* and *Air*, are compared and examined by the *Author*, 288. The result of it, 290. *Mersennus's* observation reconciled with that of the *Author*; and the proportion between the gravity of *Water* and *Air* around *London*, 291 etc. After the recital of the *Opinions* of several *Writers* regarding the proportion of gravity between *Water* and *Quicksilver*, the *Author* sets down his own trials, made in several ways, together with his conclusion from them, 293 etc. The use he makes of this inquiry for guessing at the height of the *Atmosphere*, 297. What other *Experiments* are required for the determination of it. 299 etc.

The *37th Experiment*, regarding the strange and odd *Phenomenon* of the sudden flashes of light in the cavity of the *Receiver*; the several circumstances and difficulties of it,

<meta>This page continues the dedication to the Lord of Dungarvan, following Boyle's explanation that he hopes to provide empirical proofs for theories about the air and vacuum.</meta>

consider myself, in paying a duty to you, to have performed a service to the Commonwealth of Learning. Since it may greatly contribute to the advancement of that Experimental Philosophy—the effective pursuit of which requires a purse as well as a brain—to endear it to promising persons of your rank: who may accomplish many things which others can only *wish* for or, at most, only *design*, by being able to employ the gifts of fortune in the search for the mysteries of nature.

And I am strongly induced to choose this subject, rather than any of the expected chemical ones, to entertain your Lordship, for these two considerations: The first, that the air is so necessary to human life that not only the generality of men, but most other creatures that breathe, cannot live many minutes without it; any significant discovery of its nature, therefore, seems likely to prove important to mankind. And the other is, that the surrounding air, being that with which both our own bodies and most of the others we deal with here below are almost perpetually in contact; not only its alte-

      But as few inventions happen to be at first so complete as not to be either blemished with some deficiencies needing to be remedied, or otherwise capable of improvement; so when the engine we have been speaking of comes to be more attentively considered, there will appear two very significant things to be desired in it. For first, the *Wind-Pump* (as someone not improperly calls it) is so contrived that to evacuate the vessel, the continual labor of two strong men for several hours is required. And next (which is an imperfection of much greater importance), the receiver, or glass to be emptied, consisting of one entire and uninterrupted globe and neck of glass, the whole engine is so made that things cannot be conveyed into it upon which to try experiments. So that there seems but little (if anything) more to be expected from it than those very few *phenomena* that have been already observed by the author and recorded by *Schottus*. Therefore, to remedy these inconveniences, I put both Mr. *G.*

<meta>This page continues the detailed description of the air pump engine, specifically focusing on the first of its two main components: the glass vessel or receiver.</meta>

<page-num>9</page-num>

self: It consists of two principal parts; a glass vessel, and a pump to draw the air out of it.

The former of these (which we, along with the glassmakers, shall often call a *receiver*, because of its similarity to the large vessels of that name used by *chemists*) consists of a glass with a wide hole at the top, a cover for that hole, and a stop-cock fastened to the end of the neck at the bottom.

The shape of the glass you will find illustrated in the first figure of the attached scheme. As for its size, it held about 30 wine quarts, each of them containing nearly two pounds (at 16 ounces to the pound) of water. We would have been better pleased with a more capacious vessel, but the glassmakers claimed they were unable to blow one larger that maintained the thickness and shape required for our purpose.

At the very top of the vessel (*A*), you may observe a round hole, whose diameter (*B C*) is about four inches; the orifice of which is encircled with a lip of glass almost an inch high. For the making of this lip, it was necessary (to mention this in passing, in case your

The remaining bottom-most part consists of a frame and of a sucking pump—or as we formerly called it, an air pump—supported by it: The frame is of wood, small but very strong, consisting of three legs (1 1 1), so placed that one side of it may stand perpendicular, so that the free motion of the hand may not be hindered. In the middle of which frame is transversely nailed a board (2 2 2), which may not improperly be called a midriff, upon which rests, and to which is strongly fastened, the main part of the pump itself, which is the only thing remaining to be described.

It is turned back and forth by the third piece of this pump, namely the handle or *manubrium* (7), of which the figure gives a sufficient description.

The fourth and last part of this cylinder is the valve (R), consisting of a hole bored through the top of the cylinder, tapering slightly toward the cavity; into this hole is fitted a ground, tapering brass peg, to be pushed in and taken out at will.

The engine being thus described, it will be necessary to add that something is usually done before it is put to work, to allow for the easier moving of the sucker and for the better exclusion of the outward air; for when the vessel begins to be exhausted, the air is much more difficult to keep out than one would easily imagine.

<meta>This page begins the description of the first experiment, transitioning from the general construction of the air pump to its mechanical operation and the physical effects of suction.</meta>

<page-num>20</page-num>
<meta>catchword: Receiver</meta>

<margin>
*Experiment* I.
</margin>
To proceed now to the *phenomena* exhibited to us by the engine described above; I hold it not unfit to begin with what constantly and regularly offers itself to our observation, as depending upon the construction of the engine itself, and not upon the nature of this or that particular experiment which it is employed to try.

First, then, upon the drawing down of the <term>sucker</term> <note>the piston of the pump</note> (the valve being shut), the cylindrical space vacated by the sucker is left devoid of air; and therefore, upon the turning of the key, the air contained in the <term>receiver</term> rushes into the emptied cylinder, until the air in both those vessels is brought to about an equal measure of expansion. And therefore, upon shutting the receiver by returning the key, if you open the valve and force up the sucker again, you will find that after this first suction you will drive out almost a whole cylinder full of air. But at the following suctions, you will draw less and less air out of the receiver into the cylinder, because there will still remain less and less air in the receiver.

<summary>Boyle describes the fundamental mechanical operation of his air pump, explaining how the piston (sucker) and valves work together to extract air. He observes that while the first stroke removes a large volume of air, subsequent strokes remove decreasing amounts as the air inside the receiver becomes more rarefied.</summary>

<keywords>Experiment I, air pump, vacuum, sucker, valve, receiver, cylinder, expansion of air, mechanical operation</keywords>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the first experiment, focusing on the behavior of air pressure and the physical resistance encountered when operating the air pump.</meta>

<page-num>( 21 )</page-num>

receiver itself; and consequently, the particles of the remaining air, having more room to expand, will press against one another less. You will easily perceive this by finding that you continually force less and less air out of the cylinder; so that when the receiver is almost exhausted, you may force the <term>sucker</term> <note>the pump piston</note> almost to the top of the cylinder before you will need to open the valve to let out any air. And if at such a time, with the valve being shut, you let go of the handle of the pump, you will find the sucker forcibly carried up to the top of the cylinder by the pressure of the external air; which, being much less rarefied than that within the cylinder, must exert a more powerful pressure upon the sucker than the internal air is able to resist. And by this means, you may know how far you have emptied the receiver. To this we may add, on this occasion, that constantly upon the turning of the key to let out the air from the receiver into the emptied cylinder, a considerably sharp noise is immediately produced, especially while there is still an abundance of air in the receiver.

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the physical operation of the air pump to the theoretical explanation of its effects, specifically introducing the concept of the "spring of the air."</meta>

<page-num>(22)</page-num>
<page-type>text</page-type>

For the easier understanding of the experiments that can be tested by our **engine**, I thought it was not unnecessary or ill-timed, in the description of this first one, to suggest the notion by which it seems likely that most, if not all, of them will be explained. Your Lordship will easily assume that the notion I speak of is that there is a **spring**, or elastic power, in the air we live in. By this <term>ἐλατὴρ</term> <note>Greek for "driver" or "spring"</note> or **spring of the air**, what I mean is this: that our air either consists of, or at least is full of, parts of such a nature that if they are bent or compressed by the weight of the overhead part of the **atmosphere**, or by any other body, they try as hard as they can to free themselves from that pressure by pushing against the adjacent bodies that keep them bent; and, as soon as those bodies are removed or shifted to give them room, they immediately unbend and stretch themselves out—either completely or as far as the adjacent bodies that resist them will permit—thereby expanding the whole portion of air that these elastic bodies compose.

<meta>catchword: This</meta>

<summary>Boyle introduces his central theory of the "spring of the air," explaining that air is composed of elastic particles that naturally resist compression and expand whenever external pressure is reduced.</summary>
<keywords>spring of the air, elastic power, atmosphere, air particles, compression, expansion, physics, Robert Boyle</keywords>

This notion may perhaps be somewhat 
further explained, by conceiving the 
*air* near the *earth* to be such a heap of 
little bodies, lying one upon another, as 
may be likened to a *fleece of wool*. 
For this (to omit other similarities between 
them) consists of many slender and flexible
hairs; each of which, may indeed, 
like a little *spring*, be easily bent or rolled
up; but will also, like a *spring*, be 
still endeavoring to stretch itself out

and the *aerial corpuscles* to which we

its structure) is endowed with a power or

with the nature of the body: Yet while

they compose an outward endeavor,

hand that opposes its *expansion*. And 
upon the removal of the external pressure,
by opening the hand more or less, the

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "spring of the air," comparing the expansion of compressed air to the behavior of wool and sponges, and introduces René Descartes' particle theory of air.</meta>

<page-num>(24)</page-num>
<page-type>text</page-type>

the recovery of its former looser and free condition, until the fleece has either regained its former dimensions, or at least approached them as near as the compressing hand (perhaps not quite opened) will permit. This power of self-dilation is somewhat more conspicuous in a compressed dry sponge than in a fleece of wool. But yet we rather chose to employ the latter on this occasion because it is not, like a sponge, an entire body, but a number of slender and flexible bodies, loosely intertwined, as the air itself seems to be.

There is yet another way to explain the spring of the air, namely, by supposing with that most ingenious gentleman, Monsieur *Descartes*, that the air is nothing but a *congeries* <note>A collection of items or parts in one mass.</note> or heap of small and (for the most part) flexible particles of several sizes and of all kinds of figures which are raised by heat (especially that of the sun) into that fluid and subtle ethereal body that surrounds the earth; and by the restless agitation of that celestial matter in which those particles swim, are so whirled round,

<meta>catchword: round,</meta>

<summary>Boyle explains the "spring of the air" through analogies with the elasticity of wool and sponges, then introduces Descartes' theory that air consists of diverse, flexible particles agitated by heat and celestial matter.</summary>
<keywords>spring of the air, self-dilation, fleece of wool, sponge, René Descartes, particles, celestial matter, ethereal body, physics</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of Monsieur Descartes' theory regarding the nature of air particles and their motion within the fluid ether.</meta>

<page-num>( 25 )</page-num>

round, that each corpuscle endeavors to beat off all others from coming within the little sphere requisite to its motion about its own center; and (in case any, by intruding into that sphere, shall oppose its free rotation) to expel or drive it away: So that according to this doctrine, it matters very little whether the particles of the air have the structure requisite to springs, or be of any other form (no matter how irregular), since their elastic power is not made to depend upon their shape or structure, but upon the vehement agitation and (as it were) brandishing motion which they receive from the fluid *ether* that swiftly flows between them, and whirling about each of them (independently from the rest) not only keeps those slender aerial bodies separated and stretched out (at least, as far as the neighboring ones will permit) which otherwise, by reason of their flexibility and weight, would flag or curl; but also makes them hit against and knock away each other, and consequently require more room than that which, if they were compressed, they would take up.

<summary>Boyle explains the Cartesian theory that the "spring" or elasticity of air arises from the rapid agitation of particles by fluid ether, causing them to repel one another and occupy more space.</summary>
<keywords>corpuscle, sphere of motion, rotation, elastic power, ether, aerial bodies, Descartes, particle theory, agitation</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues Robert Boyle's discussion of the two primary hypotheses for explaining the "spring" or elasticity of the air: the mechanical-structural model (comparing air particles to miniature wire springs) and the Cartesian model (based on the agitation of particles by a subtle ether), as introduced on page 25.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>
<meta>catchword: ed,</meta>

By these two different ways, my Lord, the Spring of the Air may be explained. But although I will mostly use the first of these in the following discourse because it seems somewhat easier, I am not willing to declare decisively for one over the other. And indeed, though I have in another treatise endeavored to show it is probable that the return of elastic bodies (if I may call them that) to their former position after being forcibly bent can be mechanically explained, I must confess that determining the cause is difficult. Whether the motion of restitution in bodies proceeds from this—that the parts of a body of a specific structure are put into motion by the bending of the spring—or from the effort of some subtle surrounding body whose passage may be opposed or obstructed, or whose pressure is unequally resisted because of the new shape or size that the bending of a spring may give to its pores: to determine this, I say, seems to me a matter of more difficulty than one would easily imagine at first sight. Therefore, I shall decline meddling with a subject which is much harder to be explain-

<summary>Boyle weighs two theories for the elasticity of air—structural mechanics versus the influence of a subtle surrounding fluid—but concludes that determining the exact cause of "restitution" in elastic bodies is too complex a task to undertake here.</summary>

other pneumatic engines in which the air has been compressed, and its springs violently bent by an apparent external force; upon the removal of which, it is no wonder that the air should, by the motion of restitution, expand itself until it has recovered its more natural dimensions: whereas in our above-mentioned first experiment, and in almost all others testable in our engine, it does not appear that any compression of the air preceded its spontaneous dilation or expansion of itself. But lest you should expect me to support this reasoning with experience, and lest you should object that most of the experiments that have been proposed to prove the gravity of the air have been either merely proposed or perhaps not accurately tried; I am content, before I go further, to mention here that I found a dry lamb’s bladder, containing nearly two-thirds of a pint and compressed by a packthread tied about it, lost one and one-eighth grains of its former weight by the escape of the air upon my having pricked it. And this was measured with a pair of scales which, when the full bladder and the corresponding weight were in it, would clearly tip either way with the 32nd part of a grain. And if it is further objected that the air in the bladder was violently compressed by the packthread and the sides of the bladder, we might probably (to avoid lengthy answers) be provided with a reply by recording the different weights of our <term>Receiver</term> when emptied and when full of uncompressed air, if we could obtain scales here fit for such a precise experiment;

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the weight of air, moving from the author's own experiments to those conducted in Germany, and begins to explore the height and compression of the atmosphere.</meta>

<page-num>30</page-num>

...since we are informed that in the *German* experiment, commended at the beginning of this letter, the ingenious experimenters found that their glass vessel, with a capacity of 32 measures, was lighter when the air had been drawn out of it than before, by no less than one ounce and 5/16—that is, an ounce and very nearly a third. But concerning the gravity of the air, we may have occasion to make further mention elsewhere.

Taking it for granted, then, that the air is not devoid of weight, it will not be difficult to conceive that the part of the atmosphere wherein we live—being its lower part—is composed of corpuscles that are very much compressed by the weight of all those of a similar nature directly above them. That is, by all the particles of air that, being piled up upon them, reach to the top of the atmosphere. And although the height of this *atmosphere*, according to the famous *Kepler* and some others, scarcely exceeds eight common miles, yet other eminent and more recent astronomers would extend the boundaries of the atmosphere to exceed six or seven times that number of miles. And the diligent and learned

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the height of the atmosphere, citing Riccioli's estimate, and uses the metaphor of piled wool to explain how the weight of the upper atmosphere compresses the air at the surface.</meta>

<page-num>31</page-num>

*Ricciolo* makes it probable that the atmosphere may, at least in various places, be at least 50 miles high. So that, according to a moderate estimate of the thickness of the atmosphere, we may well suppose that a column of air many miles high, pressing upon some springy corpuscles of air here below, may have weight enough to bend their little springs and keep them bent. As, to resume our former comparison: if there were fleeces of wool piled up to a mountainous height upon one another, the hairs that compose the lowermost locks which support the rest would, by the weight of all the wool above them, be as strongly compressed as if a man were to squeeze them together in his hands or employ any other such moderate force to compress them. So we need not wonder that, upon taking off the incumbent air from any parcel of the atmosphere here below, the corpuscles of which that lowest air consists should expand themselves and take up more room than before.

And if it is objected that in water the weight of the upper and of the lower part is the same, I answer that, besides that,

<page-num>(36)</page-num>

body towards the left; so that neither of them is able to move it out of its place, the pressure on all sides being reduced, as it were, to an equilibrium: so that the corpuscles of the air must sometimes be considered as little springs, which, remaining bent, are transported as a whole from place to place; as well as being considered as springs expanding, whose parts fly outward while they hardly change their overall position: just as the two ends of a bow, when shot, fly apart from each other, while the bow itself may be held firmly in the archer's hand. And that it is the equal pressure of the air on all sides upon the bodies that are in it which causes the easy yielding of its parts, may be argued from this: that if, with the help of our engine, the air is largely, though not totally, drawn away from one side of a body without being drawn away from the other, anyone who thinks they can move that body back and forth as easily as before will find themselves much mistaken.

The thing that is usually admired, and which may serve as our second Experiment, is this: That if, when the Receiver is almost empty, a bystander is asked to lift up the brass key (formerly described as a stopper in the brass cover), he will find it a very difficult thing to do so, if the vessel is well exhausted; and even when only a moderate quantity of air has been drawn out, he will, when he has lifted it up a little, so that it is somewhat loose from the sides of the lip or socket, which (with the help of a little oil) it exactly filled before, he will (I say) find it so difficult to be lifted up, that he will imagine there is some great weight fastened to the bottom of it. And so men, being unused to finding any resistance from the free air above them when lifting things up, are quick to conclude that what pushes down their hands must necessarily be some weight drawing beneath it, though they do not know where it is placed.

And that we have not misassigned the cause of this *phenomenon* seems evident enough by this: that as air is allowed to enter the receiver little by little, the weight that a man imagines his hand supports is clearly felt to decrease more and more; the internal air, by this replenishment, approaches more to an *equilibrium* with the external, until at length, as the receiver grows full of air again, the

Through several other experiments provided to us by our engine, the same concept of the great and equal pressure of the free air upon the bodies it surrounds might be demonstrated here, but we do not think it appropriate to anticipate those experiments yet. Therefore, I shall instead use a few lines to clear up a difficulty regarding this matter, which I have observed has troubled even some of the philosophical and mathematical spectators of our engine. They have wondered why we should speak of the air perfectly sealed within our receiver as if its pressure were the same as that of the atmosphere; whereas the thick and solid body of the glass, being completely impervious to air, clearly prevents the weighing pillar of the atmosphere from pressing in the slightest upon the air inside the glass, which it can nowhere touch. <meta>This page transitions from Robert Boyle's theoretical discussion of the "spring of the air" and nature's "abhorrence of a vacuum" to the practical demonstration of Experiment 3, using the Torricellian experiment as a supporting example.</meta>

<margin>Experiment 3.</margin> We will now proceed to observe that although, with the help of the handle, the Sucker is easily drawn down to the bottom of the cylinder; yet, without the help of that lever, the same effect would require a force or weight great enough to overcome the pressure of the entire atmosphere. This is because otherwise the air would not be driven out of its place when none is permitted to follow into the place vacated by the Sucker. This seems evident from the known *Torricellian Experiment*, in which, if the inverted tube of *mercury* is only 25 digits high, or slightly more, the quicksilver will not fall but will remain suspended in the tube; because it cannot press the underlying *mercury* with as great a force as does the resting column of air reaching from there to the top of the atmosphere. In contrast, if the column of *mercury* were three or four digits longer, it would overpower that of the external air and run out into the vessel of *mercury*, until the two columns reached an *equilibrium*, and no further. Therefore, we need not wonder that although the

    And so that it may appear that this plumpness of the bladder proceeded from the stronger spring of the air remaining in the bladder overcoming the weakened spring of the surrounding air remaining in the vessel; we returned the key of the stopcock, and by degrees allowed the external air to return into the receiver: Whereupon it happened, as was expected, that as the air came in from without, the disturbed air in the bladder was proportionally compressed into a narrower space, and the sides of the bladder grew flaccid, until the receiver having readmitted its usual quantity of air, the bladder appeared as full of wrinkles and cavities as before.

This experiment is much of the same nature as one said to have been made some years ago by that eminent geometrician Monsieur *Roberval*, with a carp's bladder emptied and placed into a tube, wherein the experiment *De Vacuo* was afterwards tried; this ingenious experiment of his justly deserves the thanks of those who have been, or shall be, concerned with discovering the nature of the air.

But to return to our experiment, we may take notice of this circumstance: after the receiver has been in some measure emptied, the bladder does, at each <term>exsuction</term>, swell much more conspicuously than it did at any of the first <term>exsuctions</term>. This is to such an extent that, towards the end of the pumping, not only may a great fold or cavity in the surface of the bladder be filled out by the stretching of the inward self-expanding air, but we have sometimes seen, upon the turning of the key to let the surrounding air pass out of the receiver into the cylinder—we have seen (I say) the air in the bladder suddenly expand itself so much and so briskly that it clearly lifted up some light bodies that leaned upon it,

Now, because very learned men have doubted whether the swelling of the bladder might have proceeded not from the expansion of the trapped air, but from the texture of the fibers—which, being accustomed to keep the bladder extended when the animal to whom it belonged was alive, might be supposed in our experiment to have returned, like so many springs, to their usual extent upon the removal of the surrounding air that compressed and bent them—because this, I say, has been doubted, we thought it fit to make this further trial.

And of this experiment these two *phenomena* may be noted: the one, that the bladder, at its breaking, gave a loud report, almost like a cracker <note>a firecracker</note>: and the other, that the air contained in the bladder had the power to break it with the mentioned impetuosity long before the ambient air was, all or nearly all, drawn out of the receiver.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Experiment 5, specifically addressing the practical difficulties and potential failures encountered when trying to burst bladders in a vacuum.</meta>

<page-num>(51)</page-num>

True experiments may, because of an easy mistake regarding some overlooked circumstance, be tried unsuccessfully; we will point out, on this occasion, that we often tried in vain to break bladders in the manner mentioned above. The cause of this appeared to be that the bladders we could not break—having been brought to us already blown up by those who sold them—had become dry before they reached our hands. Consequently, if we afterwards tied them very tightly, they were likely to tear and so become useless; and if we tied them only moderately tightly, their stiffness prevented them from being closed so precisely that when the contained air in the exhausted receiver had distended them as much as it easily could, it would partially escape between the small wrinkles of the sphincter of the neck. As a result, it also usually happened that, upon letting in the air from outside, the bladders appeared more flaccid and empty than before they were put in; whereas when the bladders were brought to us moist from the butchers, we could, without injuring them, tie their necks so tightly that none of the air once

<page-num>(52)</page-num>
<meta>catchword: parcel</meta>

blown in, could escape them, except by violently breaking them.

It will not be inappropriate on this occasion to point out something that may deserve more deliberate consideration than we can now give it; namely, that the *elastic power* of the same quantity of air may be increased just as well by the agitation of the *aerial particles* (whether only moving them more swiftly and scattering them, or also extending or stretching them out, I do not determine) within a body that encloses them on all sides and yet is flexible, as it is shown by the withdrawing of the air that pressed upon it from the outside. For we found that a bladder, only moderately filled with air and strongly tied, being held near the fire for a while, not only grew exceedingly swollen and hard, but afterwards, being brought closer to the fire, suddenly broke with such a loud and vehement noise that it stunned those nearby and made us, for a while afterward, almost deaf.

<margin>
Experiment 6.
</margin>
Having thus seen that the air has an elastic power, we were next desirous to know, in some measure, how far a parcel

And although we were not provided with instruments fit to measure the expansion of the air very accurately, yet because an imperfect measure of it was more desirable than none at all, we devised the following method as very easily practicable.

We took a flexible lamb's bladder which was thoroughly wetted in clean water, so that when its sides were squeezed together, there might be no air left in its folds (as indeed we could not afterwards detect any upon trial). The neck of this bladder was tied tightly around that of a small glass vial (capable of holding five full drams <note>A dram is a unit of weight or volume; in this context, it likely refers to fluid drams, approximately 3.7 milliliters each.</note> of water). The bladder was first so compressed that all the contained air was only in the glass, without being pressurized there. Then, the pump being set to work, after a few strokes, the air in the little vial began to expand itself and produce a small swelling in the neck of the bladder. As the surrounding air was more and more drawn away, the contained air penetrated further and further into the bladder, and by degrees lifted up the sides and unfolded its folds, until at length it seemed to have blown it

Then, taking out the bladder but without severing it from the glass, we filled the vessel they both made up <note>The combined volume of the vial and the expanded bladder.</note> with water through a hole made at the top of the bladder. Since we wrote the previous experiment the other day, we have found some glassware not unsuitable for our purpose; by means of which we are now able, with a little more trouble, to measure the expansion of the air much more accurately than we could with the help of the aforementioned bladder, which was much too narrow to allow the air its utmost distension.

We first took a cylindrical glass pipe, whose bore was about a quarter of an inch in diameter. This pipe was so bent and doubled that, despite being about two feet in length, it could have been shut up in a small receiver not even a foot high. But by misfortune, it cracked during cooling, whereby we were reduced to using one part which was straight and intact, but did not exceed six or seven inches. This little tube was open at one end; and at the other, where it was hermetically sealed, it had a small glass bubble to receive the air whose expansion was to be measured.

<meta>catchword: Along</meta>

Along the side of this tube was pasted a straight, narrow piece of parchment, divided into twenty-six equal parts and marked with black lines and figures, so that by them both the trapped air and its expansion might be measured. Afterwards, we filled the tube with water almost to the top; then, stopping the open end with a finger and inverting the tube, the air was allowed to ascend to the aforementioned glass bubble. And because this ascent was very slow, it gave us the opportunity to observe whether this air occupied more or less than one of the twenty-six divisions. By this means, after a trial or two, we were enabled to convey a bubble of air to the top of the glass that was, to the eye, equal to one of those divisions. It has seemed almost incredible what is related by the industrious *Mersennus*: that air, by the violence of heat (even as great as our vessels can support without melting), can be so dilated as to take up seventy times as much room as before. Therefore, because we were willing to have confirmation of such a strange *phenomenon*, we once more conveyed into the tube a bubble the size of the former one; and carrying out the experiment as before with the same water, we observed that the air manifestly stretched itself so far as to appear several times a good way below the surface of the water in the vial, and that too with a surface very convex toward the bottom of the pipe. We add these circumstances partly so that the *phenomenon* we have been relating may not be attributed to the mere subsiding of the water that filled the tube upon the taking off of the pressure of the surrounding air. And partly also so that it may appear that even if our experiments have not been as accurately made as might perhaps be possible with better instruments, yet the expansion of the air is likely to be rather greater than less than we have measured it. Since the air was able to push away the water at the bottom of the pipe, though that was about two inches below the surface of the water that was then in the vial, and would have been at least as high in the pipe if the water had only subsided and not been depressed. So it seems not unlikely that if the experiment could be so made that the expansion of the air might not be resisted by neighboring bodies, it would enlarge its bounds even further, and perhaps stretch itself to two hundred times its former bulk, if not more. However, what we have now tried will, I hope, suffice to prevent various *phenomena* of our engine from being mistrusted:

<margin>Experiment 9.</margin> We took a glass vial capable of holding three or four ounces of water, and of the usual thickness for glasses of that size; into the neck of this was put a moderately slender glass pipe, which was carefully fastened with a mixture of equal parts of pitch and rosin to the neck of the vial, and which reached almost to the bottom of it, as the sixth figure shows.

But it seemed that in such a small receiver, the air around the vial being suddenly drawn out, the air imprisoned in the vessel—having on it the whole pressure of the atmosphere (to which it and the water were exposed by the pipe open at both ends) and not having on the other side the usual pressure of the ambient air to balance that other pressure—finally overcame the resistance of the glass. Once the vial began to break at its weakest point, the external air might have rushed in with enough violence to throw the cracked piece so forcibly against the neighboring side of the receiver as to break that as well.

And this may be presumed sufficient to verify what we stated in that part of our Appendix to the first Experiment, where we mentioned the almost equal pressure of the air on either side of a thin glass vessel as the cause of its not being broken by the strong spring of the contained air. <meta>This page continues the discussion of Experiment 9, specifically concluding the observation that thicker glasses do not shatter under air pressure, thus refuting the idea of an "abhorrence of a vacuum" as an infinite force. most boundless power; we afterwards purposely tried this *Experiment* with several glasses somewhat thicker than those vials, and found the outcome to verify our conjecture that it would not succeed: for the glasses were taken out as entire as they were put in.

And here, *My Lord*, I hold it not inappropriate, upon the occasion of the mention that has been made of our having employed small *Receivers*, and one of them despite its being cracked, to add these two *Advertisements* <note>notes or observations</note>.

First then, besides the great *Pneumatical Glass* so often mentioned, and the proportionate *Stop-cock*, we thought it fit to provide ourselves with some small *Receivers* blown of *Crystalline Glass*, of several shapes, and furnished with smaller *Stop-cocks* purposely made; and this we did in the hopes that once we had overcome the difficulties met with in cementing the glasses to the *Stop-cocks*, and the *Pneumatical Vessels* to the *Pump* as precisely as is required for our purpose, we should, from the smallness of our *Receivers*, receive a fourfold *Advantage*. The first, that by reason of the slenderness of the *Vessels*, and their be-

<summary>The author concludes that thicker glass vials do not break under air pressure, proving the force is limited. He then begins to describe the advantages of using smaller, specially-made crystalline receivers in pneumatic experiments.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the advantages of using smaller Receivers in pneumatic experiments, following from the previous page's description of their construction from thin, clear crystalline glass.</meta>

ing made of much purer and clearer metal, as the glassmakers speak, than the great Receiver, we might have a more perfect view of everything happening within them. The next, that such small vessels might be emptied with less labor and in much less time. The third, that this nimble suction of the ambient air would make many changes in the bodies shut up in these glasses more sudden and conspicuous than otherwise they would prove. And the last, that we should be able to draw and keep out the air much more perfectly from such small vessels than from our large Receiver. But though we were not much disappointed in the expectation of the first three advantages, yet we were in our hopes of the fourth. For besides the great difficulty we found in fitting together the glasses, the stopcocks and the covers; besides this I say, we found ourselves rarely able to draw, and keep out the air so far as to make the remaining air in these Receivers weaker than the remaining air in our great Receiver. For though sometimes the leaks of some of these little Receivers may be much either fewer or smaller than those of the larger vessel; yet a little air get-

The other thing that we were to point out is that it is not every small crack that makes such a receiver of a roundish figure altogether useless to our experiment, considering that upon the suction of the internal air, the ambient air on all sides pressing the glass inwards or towards the middle, consequently thrusts the lips of the crack closer, and so closes it rather than increasing it.

I mention this partly because receivers suitable for our purpose are more easily cracked than obtained, and therefore ought not to be unnecessarily thrown away as useless. And partly because I think it becomes one who professes himself a faithful relator of experiments not to conceal from Your Lordship that after a few of the previous experiments were made, there occurred in the large receiver a crack about a span long, begin-

The plaster was made of good quicklime finely powdered, and nimbly ground with a pestle in a mortar, with a quantity (I know not how much precisely, not having those essays in this place) of scrapings of cheese and a little pure water—no more than is just necessary to bring the mixture to a somewhat soft paste—which, when the ingredients are perfectly incorporated, will have a strong and stinking smell. Then it must be immediately spread upon a linen cloth three or four fingers wide, and instantly applied, lest it begin to harden. But if Your Lordship had seen how we mended

<margin>Experiment 10.</margin>
We took a tallow candle of such a size that eight of them make about a pound, and having lowered it into the receiver in a very convenient candlestick, and suspended it so that the flame burned almost in the middle of the vessel, we precisely sealed it up in about two minutes. And, upon pumping very quickly, we found that the flame went out within a little more than half a minute, even though the wick had been purposely left at the length we judged most convenient for the flame to last.

But the second time, having put the same candle into the receiver (after it had been cleared of fumes by blasts from a pair of bellows), the flame lasted about two minutes from the time the pumper began to draw out the air; upon the first suction of which, the flame seemed to contract itself in all its dimensions. And although we were led by that to suppose it was completely extinguished, we continued pumping for a while in pursuit of another experiment we were trying at the same time. And we did this more willingly because of a suspicion that the experiment with the coals might suggest, which the result showed was not entirely groundless. For upon the admission of the external air, the fire, which seemed to have gone out a considerable time before, immediately revived; and being, as it were, refreshed by the new air and blown by the wind made by that air rushing in, it began to shine again and dissipate the neighboring fuel into smoke as before.

<margin>
Experiment 13.
</margin>

A short time later, we lowered into the Receiver, together with a lighted piece of match, a large bladder that was well-tied at the neck but very limp, as it did not actually contain much (if anything) more than a pint of air, though it was capable of holding ten or twelve times as much.

Our goal in this experiment was partly to test whether or not the smoke from the match, filling the Receiver, would be able to hinder the expansion of the internal air [within the bladder] upon the suction of the surrounding air. And it was partly to discover whether the extinguishing of the fire on the match resulted from a lack of air, or merely from the pressure of its own fumes, which—for lack of room to expand—might be supposed to recoil back upon the fire and so stifle it.

The outcome of our trial was that at the beginning of our pumping, the match appeared well-lit, though it had almost filled the Receiver with its plentiful fumes. But by degrees it burnt more and more dimly, despite the fact that by the quick drawing

<summary>Boyle begins Experiment 13 by placing a limp bladder and a burning match inside the Receiver to see if the match's smoke prevents the bladder from expanding or if the smoke itself stifles the flame. He observes that the match begins to dim as the air is pumped out, despite the removal of some fumes.</summary>

<page-num>( 85 )</page-num>
<meta>This page continues the observation of Experiment 13, where Boyle investigates the relationship between air and combustion by using a burning match inside the vacuum receiver.</meta>

...out the air and smoke, the vessel was made less opaque and less full of compressing matter. This appeared from the fact that the longer we pumped, the less air and smoke came out of the cylinder at the opening of the valve, and consequently, the less air had entered it previously; yet the fire in the match went out only slowly. 

And when afterward, to satisfy ourselves of its extinction, we had darkened the room and in vain endeavored to discover any spark of fire—just as we could not for some time before, even with the help of candles, discern the slightest rising of smoke—we yet continued pumping six or seven times. After all that, upon letting in the air, the seemingly dead fire quickly revived and manifested its recovery by light and an abundance of smoke, with the latter of which it quickly began to refill the receiver. 

Then we began pumping once more and continued that labor until the rekindled match went out again. Thinking it then appropriate not to cease pumping as soon as before, we found that in less than half a quarter of an hour <note>approximately seven and a half minutes</note> the fire was extinguished for good and all,

Since writing these last lines, we took a small receiver, capable of containing (by estimation) about a pound and a half of water; and in the middle of it we suspended a lighted match. But although within one minute (or thereabouts) of putting in the match, we had cemented on the cover, we could not work fast enough to prevent the smoke from filling that small receiver and—as far as we could tell—choking the fire before we began to pump. Having repeated the experiment again and again, it still seemed as it did at first: we could not close up the vessel and pump out all the fumes quickly enough to save the fire from going out. Whereupon we used this expedient: as soon as we had pumped once or twice, we suddenly turned the key and thereby allowed the outside air to enter. Rushing in violently, as if it had been forced through a pair of bellows, this air both blew away the ashes, filled the glass with fresh air, and

but that some of them moved upwards, as well as some of them downwards, and some of them sideways, as they are accustomed to do when they fly out in the open *air* upon such collisions.

We also caused a piece of *steel* to be made in the shape and size of the *flint*, in whose place we put it; and then, the *pistol* being cocked and placed into the *receiver*, the *trigger* was pulled after the *air* was drawn out. And although the place was purposely darkened somewhat, yet there appeared not the least spark of *fire* upon the striking of the two *steels* against each other. Nor did we expect any (having previously attempted in vain to strike *fire* this way in the open *air*), though we thought it fit to conduct the *experiment* to undeceive those who imagine that in rarefied *air* there is some unknown strange disposition to catch *fire* upon a much slighter occasion than this *experiment* provided. We have indeed found that by the dexterous *collision* of two hardened pieces of *steel*, an abundance of sparks may be struck out; but that was done with such vehement percussion of the edges of the two *steels* as could not easily be accomplished in our *receiver*.

<meta>catchword: But</meta>

<summary>Boyle observes that sparks in a vacuum move in various directions just as they do in the open air, and he disproves the idea that rarefied air has a special property that makes it easier to produce fire by striking steel against steel.</summary>
<keywords>Robert Boyle, vacuum, sparks, steel, flint, receiver, rarefied air, Experiment 14, percussion, physics</keywords>

But the chief thing we designed to do with our *pistol* was to observe whether *gunpowder* would take *fire* in our emptied and closely stopped *glass*? Whether the expansion of the *flame* would be considerably varied by the absence of so much of the ambient *air* as was drawn out of the *receiver*? And whether the *flame* would diffuse itself upward, as it is accustomed, notwithstanding its not having about it the usual proportion of *air* to force it up? And although most of our attempts to fire the *gunpowder* in the *pan* of the *pistol* did not succeed, because we were obliged to let it hang almost perpendicular in the *receiver*, whereby the *powder* was shaken down before the sparks could reach it; yet once the *experiment* succeeded, and the kindled *powder* seemed to make a more expanded *flame* than it would have done in the open *air*, but mounted upwards according to its custom; whether by reason of that little portion of *air* which, in spite of our pumping, remained in the *receiver*, or for any other cause, we do not now have the leisure to consider. But we must not forget that upon the extinction of the *flame*, the *receiver* appeared darkened with

<margin>Experiment 15.</margin>
**TO** these experiments concerning fire we added another, which, though it did not succeed, may perhaps be recorded without impertinence: partly because (as we have amply declared in another treatise) it is useful to recount which experiments miscarry as well as those that succeed. And partly also because it is very possible that what we endeavored in vain may be performed by Your Lordship, or some other *virtuoso* that shall have tighter <note>stancker: more airtight or sturdier</note> vessels than we had, and more sunny days than the present winter allows us.

<margin>Experiment 16.</margin> We planned to try whether or not various magnetic experiments would exhibit any unusual *phenomena* if they were conducted in our evacuated receiver instead of the open air. But for lack of leisure and convenience to pursue such trials, we were led to save the rest for another time and to content ourselves with conducting the experiment that follows. We placed into the receiver a little pedestal of wood, in the middle of which a slender iron pin was perpendicularly erected. Upon its sharp point, a magnetized needle of steel, made for this purpose and about five inches long, was placed so that, hanging in *equilibrium*, it could move freely toward either side. Then, after the air was pumped out in the usual manner, we applied a moderately powerful lodestone to the outside of the glass. We found that it attracted or repelled the ends of the needle according to the laws of magnetism, without any remarkable difference from what the same lodestone would have done had

<margin>Experiment 17.</margin>

From this, I inferred (as I easily might) that if the experiment could be tried in our engine, the quicksilver would sink below 27 digits in proportion to the suction of air that should be made out of the receiver. For, just as when the air is shut into the receiver, it does (according to what has been taught above) continue there as strongly compressed as it did while the entire pressing column of the atmosphere leaned directly upon it; because the glass in which it is trapped prevents it from releasing itself, through an expansion of its parts, from the pressure with which it was shut up. So, if we could perfectly draw the air out of the receiver, it would serve our purpose just as well as if we were allowed to try the experiment beyond the atmosphere.

And here, to illustrate the preceding experiment, it will not be amiss to mention some other details relating to it.
First then, when we endeavored to conduct the experiment with the tube closed at one end with *Diachylon* <note>a type of adhesive plaster made of lead oxide and oil</note> instead of a hermetic seal, we perceived that upon drawing some of the air out of the receiver, the mercury did indeed begin to fall, but continued to subside afterward, even though we did not continue pumping. From this it appeared that although the *Diachylon* that stopped the end of the tube was so thick and strong that the external air could not press it in (as experience taught us that it would have done if there had been but little of it), yet the subtler parts of the air were able, though slowly, to work their way through the very body of the plaster; which, it seems, was of such a close texture as that which we mentioned ourselves to have successfully used in the experiment *De Vacuo* <note>Latin: On the Vacuum</note> some years ago. So that now we begin to suspect that perhaps one reason why we cannot perfectly pump out the air may be that when the vessel
