--- Page 151 ---
<meta>This page continues Robert Boyle's discussion on the nature of the vacuum within his air pump's receiver, exploring whether light and magnetism prove the presence of some form of matter even after the air is removed.</meta>

<page-num>(120)</page-num>

For on the one hand it appears that, notwithstanding the suction <note>exsuction</note> of the air, our receiver may not be devoid of all bodies, since anything placed in it may be seen there; which would not be the case if it were not pervious to those beams of light which, rebounding from the seen object to our eyes, affect us with the sense of it. And that either these beams are corporeal emanations from some lucid body, or else at least the light they convey results from the brisk motion of some subtle matter, I could (if I am not mistaken) sufficiently demonstrate from the dialogues mentioned above, if I thought your Lordship could seriously imagine that light could be conveyed without at least having (if I may so speak) a body for its vehicle.

By the sixteenth experiment, it also appears that the closeness of our receiver does not prevent it from admitting the effluvia of the loadstone; which makes it very probable that it also freely admits the magnetic steams of the earth; concerning which, we have in another treatise endeavored to demonstrate that many of them always permeate our air.

But on the other hand, it may be said,

<meta>catchword: That</meta>

<summary>Boyle argues that the receiver is likely not a perfect vacuum because light and magnetic forces can still pass through it, suggesting the presence of "subtle matter" or "effluvia." He posits that light requires a physical "vehicle" and notes that the receiver does not block the earth's magnetic influence.</summary>

<keywords>vacuum, receiver, light, subtle matter, magnetism, loadstone, effluvia, physics, Robert Boyle</keywords>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page continues Robert Boyle’s argument that although light and magnetism can pass through an evacuated receiver, they do not "fill" the space in a way that prevents it from being considered a vacuum. He compares the density of these subtle particles to the way a small piece of solid material occupies very little space compared to the smoke it produces.</meta>

<page-num>(121)</page-num>

That as for the subtle matter which makes the objects enclosed in our evacuated receiver visible, and the magnetic effluvia of the earth that may be presumed to pass through it: even if we should grant that our vessel is not quite devoid of them, we cannot so reasonably affirm it to be replenished with them. We may instead suppose that if they were gathered together into one place without spaces between them, they would fill only a small part of the entire receiver. As in the thirteenth experiment, a piece of match was insignificant in its bulk while its parts lay close together, but afterwards (when the fire had scattered them into smoke) it seemed to fill the entire vessel. For (as our experiments elsewhere have demonstrated) both light and the effluvia of the loadstone may be readily admitted into a glass, hermetically sealed, even though before their admission it was as full of air as hollow bodies here below are accustomed to be. So, upon the suction <note>exsuction</note> of the air, the large space vacated by it may remain empty, despite the presence of those subtle corpuscles by which luminous and magnetic bodies produce their effects.

<meta>catchword: And</meta>

<summary>Boyle argues that even if light and magnetic particles exist within the evacuated receiver, they occupy so little actual space that the container can still be considered a vacuum. He illustrates this by comparing the small volume of a solid match to the large volume of smoke it generates.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Boyle's argument regarding whether an evacuated receiver is truly empty or if it is filled with a "subtle matter." He moves from discussing magnetic and light particles to addressing the philosophical claims of the "Plenists."</meta>

<page-num>(122)</page-num>

And as for the allegations mentioned above, they seem to prove only that the receiver, when devoid of air, *may be* replenished with some such ethereal matter as some modern naturalists write of, but not that it really *is* so. And indeed, it still seems to me that regarding those spaces which the <term>*Vacuists*</term> claim to be empty because they are manifestly devoid of air and all grosser bodies, the <term>*Plenists*</term> (if I may call them that) do not prove that such spaces are replenished with the subtle matter they speak of by any sensible effects or operations of it (of which various new trials purposely made have not yet shown me any); they only conclude that there must be such a body because there cannot be a vacuum. And the reason why there cannot be a vacuum is taken by them, not from any experiments or *phenomena* of nature that clearly and particularly prove their *hypothesis*, but from their notion of a body; according to them, the nature of a body consists only in <term>extension</term> (which indeed seems the property most essential to, because it is inseparable from, a body). To say there is a space devoid of a body is, to use the schoolmen's phrase, a <term>*Contradiction in Adjecto*</term> <note>a contradiction in terms</note>: This

<meta>catchword: rested</meta>

Reason, I say, being thus derived, seems to make the controversy about a *vacuum* rather a metaphysical than a physiological question; which, therefore, we shall no longer debate here, finding it very difficult either to satisfy naturalists with this *Cartesian* notion of a body, or to show wherein it is erroneous and substitute a better in its place.

But though we are unwilling to examine any further the inferences usually made from the *Torricellian* experiment, yet we think it not irrelevant to present Your Lordship with a couple of notices concerning it.

First, then, if in trying the experiment here or elsewhere, you make use of the *English* measures that mathematicians and tradesmen are accustomed to employ here, you will, unless you are forewarned of it, be likely to suspect that those who have written about the experiment have been mistaken. Although we did not think it improbable that the difference between the thicker English air and that of *Italy* and *France* might prevent the quicksilver from falling quite as low in this colder climate as in those warmer ones, we could not believe that this difference in the air alone could cause such a great difference in the heights of the mercurial cylinders. Accordingly, upon inquiry, we found that although the varying density of the air should not be overlooked in this experiment, the main reason why we found the cylinder of mercury to consist of so many inches was this: that our English inches are somewhat shorter in length than the digits used in foreign parts by the writers of the experiment.

And since we have come to consider the height of the mercurial cylinder, I must not hide from Your Lordship an experiment related to it, which will perhaps set both you and many of your friends, the *virtuosi*, to thinking; and, by disclosing some things about the air or atmosphere that have scarcely been noticed until now, may afford you some hints conducive to a further discovery of the subject of this letter.

---

<meta>This page continues the discussion of an experiment suggested by Mr. Wren to observe if mercury levels fluctuate in accordance with the tides.</meta>

<meta>catchword: If</meta>

Spring-Tides. For he sagaciously and plausibly conjectured that such observations, accurately made, would discover the truth or erroneousness of the *Cartesian Hypothesis* concerning the ebbing and flowing of the sea: which *Descartes* ascribes to the greater pressure made upon the air by the moon, and the intercurrent ethereal substance at certain times (of the day, and of the lunar month) than at others. But since we found the quicksilver in the tube to move up and down so uncertainly, by reason, as it seems, of accidental changes in the air; I somewhat doubt whether we shall find the altitude of the quicksilver to vary as regularly as the experiment is ingeniously proposed. The success we shall (God permitting us to make a trial of it) acquaint Your Lordship with; and in the meantime take notice, that when we had occasion to take the tube out of the frame (after it had stayed there part of *November* and part of *December*) a good fire being then in the room, because it was a snowy day, we found the quicksilver in the tube to be 29 and three-quarter inches above the upper surface of the mercury below it.

--- Page 165 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's investigation into the behavior of mercury in a vacuum tube (a barometer), specifically addressing the physical causes behind the fluctuations in the height of the mercury column.</meta>

(134)

If your Lordship should now ask me what the true causes are for this varying height <note>original: "altitude"</note> of the mercury column <note>original: "Mercurial Cylinder"</note>, I would not attempt to answer such a difficult question. I would venture to say no more than that among various possible causes to which it may be attributed, it would not be absurd, perhaps, to consider the following.

First, we may consider that the air in the upper part of the tube is much more rarefied <note>thinned out or made less dense</note>, and therefore weaker than the external air. This is evident from several things, including that when the tube is tilted, the mercury <note>original: "Quick-silver"</note> will readily rise almost to the very top. It fills eight or nine-tenths—and perhaps more—of the space it had previously vacated. This would not happen if that entire space had been full of unrarefied air, since that air (as an experiment <note>original: "tryal"</note> may easily satisfy you) would not have allowed itself to be forced into such a small space by such weak pressure. So, although the mercury in our tube was somewhat lowered <note>original: "depress’d"</note> when the trapped air was heated, there is still a difference between such a tube and common weather-glasses <note>early scientific instruments, such as air thermometers, used to observe atmospheric changes</note>. In those instruments, the trapped and the surrounding <margin>ambient</margin>

<summary>The author discusses why the mercury column in a barometer varies in height, suggesting that the air trapped at the top of the tube is significantly less dense (rarefied) than the air outside.</summary>
<keywords>barometer, mercury, vacuum, rarefaction, atmospheric pressure, Robert Boyle, weather-glasses, physics</keywords>

--- Page 166 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison between the trapped air in a mercury barometer and the air in a standard weather-glass (thermometer), explaining how the weight of the liquid and the pressure of the atmosphere determine the height of the column.</meta>

(135)

air are in an equilibrium <note>original: "Æquilibrium"</note> of pressure, and the weight of the water that keeps them separate is barely significant <note>original: "scarce considerable"</note>. However, in a tube like the one we are discussing, the air inside is much more expanded <note>original: "dilated"</note> than the air outside. It is not so much the elasticity <note>original: "spring"</note> or resistance of the trapped <note>original: "included"</note> air, but rather the weight of the mercury column <note>original: "Mercurial Cylinder"</note> itself that prevents the mercury <note>original: "Quick-silver"</note> from rising higher. 

For if we were to assume that the empty <note>original: "deserted"</note> part of the tube was perfectly devoid of air, the mercury would still rise only a little higher and would be far from filling the space. This is because the outside air would not be able to push <note>original: "impel"</note> such a weight much higher. On the other hand, it appears from our former experiments that if all the air in the upper part of a weather-glass <note>an early scientific instrument, such as an air thermometer, used to observe atmospheric changes</note> were removed, the water would be pushed up to the very top, even if the pipe were more than thirty feet long.

We may next consider that this rarefied <note>thinned or less dense</note> air at the top of our tube, being tightly sealed between the glass and the mercury, was hardly subject to any noticeable <note>original: "discernable"</note> changes, except for those caused by heat and cold.

<summary>The author explains that the height of mercury in a barometer is limited by the metal's weight and atmospheric pressure, unlike in a water-based instrument where water would fill the entire tube. He also notes that the rarefied air trapped in the tube is primarily affected by temperature changes.</summary>
<keywords>atmospheric pressure, mercury, barometer, rarefaction, weather-glass, equilibrium, physics, Robert Boyle</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the atmosphere is subject to change, shifting focus from temperature to other factors like density and altitude that affect barometric readings.</meta>

(136)

And we may further consider that the external air or atmosphere is still subject to many alterations, besides those that result from either of those qualities <note>referring to heat and cold mentioned on the previous page</note>.

For the experiment that prompted <note>original: "occasion'd"</note> this discourse seems to make it probable enough that there may be strange ebbings and flowings, so to speak, in the atmosphere. Or at least, it may undergo great and sudden changes <note>original: "Mutations"</note>, either in its altitude or its density, due to causes that are as unknown to us as their effects are unnoticed <note>original: "unheeded"</note> by us. And so that you do not think our experiment is the only thing in nature that supports this conjecture, we might remind Your Lordship <note>likely a reference to one of Boyle's patrons, such as Lord Dungarvan or Lord Richard Jones</note> of the pains and aches often complained of by those who have had severe wounds or bruises. These pains often predict <note>original: "presage"</note> great changes in the air, while no sign of such a thing appears to strong and healthy people.

And there is another very memorable point for this purpose, which I remember reading somewhere in a book by the ingenious **Kircher** <note>original: "Kircherus"; Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680) was a celebrated German Jesuit scholar and polymath who wrote extensively on science and technology</note>. While giving a relevant warning <note>original: "pertinent admonition"</note> concerning the various refractions <note>the bending of light as it passes through the air</note> that can happen in the air, he relates that...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the atmospheric phenomenon of refraction, where changes in the air's density allow distant objects to become visible or invisible to the naked eye.</meta>

During his stay in **Malta**, he often saw **Mount Etna**, though the next day, despite the weather being extremely clear, he could not see it. He added that **Vintemillius** <note>Francesco Ventimiglia, a Sicilian scholar and contemporary of the author</note>, a very learned person, often beheld from a hill he mentions the entire island he calls **Luprica** <note>Likely Lipari, a volcanic island north of Sicily</note> rising <note>original: "protuberant"</note> above the sea, even though at other times, despite a clear sky, he could not see it.

And while this might be partly attributed to the varying light and position of the sun, or to the different condition of the spectator's eye, or perhaps to some other cause, the most probable cause seems to be the differing **density of the air**. This change is caused by **exhalations** <note>vapors or gases rising from the earth or sea</note> capable of increasing **refraction** <note>the bending of light as it passes through the atmosphere, which can make objects below the horizon appear visible</note>, and consequently bringing light beams to the eye that would otherwise not reach it.

In another of our writings <note>original: "Treatise"</note>, we have also mentioned that we have often observed with **telescopes** many vapors <note>original: "steams"</note> in the air which would not be noticed without such assistance. Just as these vapors were not always visible even through a telescope, they sometimes disappeared quickly, especially after a rain shower. And when we have visited places that abound with **mines**, we have been told several times...

And I think it will easily be granted that the rising of such vapors into various parts of the air, and their mixing with it, is very likely to thicken it; just as, on the other hand, either heat or the sudden condensation of the air in another part of the *atmosphere* (to mention no other causes for now) are capable of **rarefying** <note>making the air thinner or less dense</note> it.

Nor will it greatly affect the main point of our **discussion** <note>original: "Discourse"</note> whether it is assumed that the abundant vapors the earth sends into the air thicken that part of the *atmosphere* which receives them and make it heavier; or that sometimes these fumes may rise with such **speed** <note>original: "celerity"</note> that, although the air is thickened, they actually decrease rather than increase its **weight** <note>original: "gravitation"</note>. Your Lordship may also be pleased to remember that by our seventeenth experiment, it appeared that when the air in the receiver <term>receiver</term> <note>the glass vessel of an air pump from which air is evacuated</note> was expanded more than ordinarily, the quicksilver <note>original: "Quick-silver"; mercury</note> in the tube dropped proportionally. ...or cold. Since also, according to the simple density or rarity of the air pressing down on the underlying mercury <note>original: "Quick-silver"</note> in the vessel, the mercury in the tube was pushed higher or lower; such changes occurring in the nearby outdoor air—caused either by the rising of thick or plentiful vapors <note>original: "gross or copious exhalations"</note> or by any other cause (of which there may be many)—could significantly alter the consistency of the air in terms of its rarity and density. Therefore, these changes *may* be able to proportionally alter the height of the mercury.

I say that such alterations *may* be the cause, rather than that they definitely *are* the cause of our *phenomenon* <note>the observation of mercury levels rising and falling in a tube due to atmospheric pressure</note>. I believe it is sufficient to have proposed conjectures that are not entirely irrational about a new mystery of nature. Regarding this mystery, my main goal is to encourage the curious to investigate it further than I have yet been able to do.

Experiment 20.

That air has a notable elastic <note>original: "Elastical"</note> power (wherever that comes from), we have, I believe, abundantly proven <note>original: "evinc'd"</note>, and it is beginning to be acknowledged by the most eminent modern naturalists. But whether or not there is even a weak <note>original: "languid"</note> elastic power in water seems to have been scarcely considered until now. To my knowledge, no writer has yet determined this one way or the other, which prompted <note>original: "invited"</note> us to conduct the following experiment.

Now, regarding this *Experiment*, there are two or three *details* <note>original: "Circumstances"</note> yet to be mentioned which are no less relevant to our current purpose than those already described <note>original: "recited"</note>.

This seemed to happen because their size was so great that they could not fully expand within the hollow <note>original: "cavity"</note> of the *Glass* without pressing against both sides of it; because of this, it was more difficult for the *Water* to push them upward <note>original: "extrusion to the Water"</note>.

Some of them were equal in volume to four or five *Peas*. Whether this increase in their size resulted from the greater decrease in the pressure of the *Air*,

Another thing we noted in our bubbles was that, whereas in ordinary ones the *Air*—together with the thin film of *Water* that surrounds and holds it <note>original: "invests and detains"</note>—usually swells above the surface of the *Water* it floats on, commonly forming hemispherical shapes with it, the small pockets <note>original: "parcels"</note> of *Air* that rose up after the *Receiver* <note>the glass chamber of the vacuum pump</note> was mostly emptied did not form bulging <note>original: "protuberant"</note> bubbles. However, as we noted earlier, when the external *Air* was let back in, these swollen <note>original: "tumid"</note> bubbles immediately shrank back to their former tiny size <note>original: "inconspicuousness"</note>.

All these findings taken together seem to sufficiently confirm what logic itself would suggest: that *Air* and similar substances under *Water* are pressed upon by the *Atmosphere* as well as by the weight of the *Water* sitting on top of them.

Experiment—namely, that from the mere swelling of water recorded there, it cannot be safely concluded that water, when freed from compression, possesses an elastic power <note>original: "Elastical power"</note> of expanding itself. It appears that the swelling <note>original: "Intumescence"</note> produced by that experiment may (at least for the most part) be attributed to the numerous little bubbles that are usually produced in water once the pressure of the atmosphere is largely removed. We are so easily misled into mistakes—even by experiments themselves—when we either fail to consider that most effects can arise from various causes, or when we only pay attention to those circumstances of our experiment which seem to agree with our preconceived *hypothesis* <note>a proposed explanation or theory</note> or conjectures.

Because of this, it also seems probable that within the pores or tiny, invisible gaps <note>original: "recesses"</note> of water itself, there are commonly scattered many pockets <note>original: "parcels"</note> of either air, or at least something similar to it <note>original: "Analogous thereunto"</note>. It may indeed, as we recently suggested, be conjectured that these bubbles do not come so much from any air already existing in the water and lurking in its pores, but rather from the more subtle parts of the water itself. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;From this, it seems very probable, if not certain, that even in the densest and heaviest liquids <note>original: "ponderous Liquors"</note>, and therefore even more so in water, there may hide undetectable pockets of air. power in the water. It might be argued that the swelling of the compressed water in the pewter vessel mentioned earlier—and the way the water sprayed out from the hole made by the needle—were not caused by any internal elasticity <term>elater: an archaic term for elasticity or the inherent power of a substance to expand</term> of the water itself. Instead, these effects resulted from the springiness <note>original: "spring"</note> of many tiny particles of air dispersed throughout that water. These air particles acted upon the water as they suddenly expanded back to their natural size after being released from the extreme compression that had squeezed them down.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;However, even though all these details make it clear that the bubbles we have been discussing were produced by a substance that can properly be called air, I will not yet definitively decide whether air is a primary <term>primogenial body: a fundamental, original element that cannot be broken down or changed into something else</term> substance. I cannot yet say for certain if air is something that cannot be created or transformed into water or any other substance, at least until we have the chance to conduct more experiments <note>original: "tryals"</note> concerning its nature. I recall, as I said, making this experiment once; but I should add that my main reason for mentioning it is to show Your Lordship <note>Boyle often addressed his works to his nephew, Lord Dungarvan</note> how necessary it is to be cautious and thoughtful when performing and drawing conclusions from precise <note>original: "nice"</note> experiments. For although it might seem that I used enough caution, I later realized that the aeolipile I used was very large, and that it requires much more care than someone who hasn't tried it might expect...

<meta>This page continues Robert Boyle's skeptical analysis of an experiment involving an aeolipile, where he questions whether the substance inflating a bladder is actually "new" air created from water or merely residual air trapped in the device.</meta>

(169)

one would imagine it possible to drive out all the **air** from a large **aeolipile** <note>original: "Æolipile"; a hollow metal globe with a thin neck used to demonstrate steam power</note>. However, I easily suspected that the expansion of the **bladder** in our **pneumatic vessel** <note>The receiver or vacuum chamber of Boyle's air pump</note> might not proceed from the water vapor that came out of the narrow mouth of the aeolipile (which had significantly wetted the bladder). Instead, it might come from the rarefied air which, in those sorts of vessels, is accustomed to come out alongside the rarefied water for a considerable time. 

Accordingly, having repeated the experiment, I found it very difficult to perform it accurately enough to conclude that **water** may be rarefied into true air. This difficulty was due to the shrinking of the bladders when they were heated and other obstacles.

Regarding the four other considerations mentioned above, we cannot spend time framing objections. Instead, we must proceed immediately to mention those things that seem to argue that **air** (at least the kind that produced our bubbles) may be generated from **water** and other **substances** <note>original: "Bodies"</note>.

First, we have found by experience that a vaporous air <note>original: "vapid Air"</note>, or water rarefied into **vapor**, may—at least for a while—imitate the **elastic power** <note>The "spring" or pressure of air</note> of that which is generally acknowledged to be true air.

Finally, to the previous arguments based on experience, we could easily add the authority of *Aristotle* and his followers (the Scholastics <note>original: "the Schools"; the traditional medieval universities following Aristotelian philosophy</note>), who are known to have taught that air and water, being "symbolizing elements" <term>symbolizing elements</term> <note>In Aristotelian physics, elements that shared a common quality—in this case, moisture—were thought to be easily transformed into one another</note> (sharing the quality of moisture), are easily transformed into one another.

<summary>Boyle concludes that his experiments prove air can be newly generated through chemical reactions and begins an argument for this based on "corpuscularian" philosophy—the idea that all matter is made of the same tiny particles rearranged into different forms.</summary>

--- Page 211 ---
<meta>This page continues Robert Boyle’s argument regarding the generation of air. Having previously discussed chemical experiments, he now applies the mechanical philosophy of the era—specifically Cartesian physics—to explain how the physical structure of matter might change to become air.</meta>

<page-num>(180)</page-num>

...there is no reason why the minute parts of water and other bodies may not be so agitated or connected as to deserve the name "air." For if we allow the *Cartesian hypothesis* <note>The physical theories of René Descartes, who proposed that the universe was filled with a "plenum" of matter rather than a vacuum</note>, according to which (as we noted at the beginning of this letter) the air may consist of any earthy <note>original: "terrene"</note> or watery <note>original: "aqueous"</note> corpuscles <term>corpuscles</term> <note>Small particles or atoms that make up a larger body</note>, provided they are kept swimming in the flowing <note>original: "interfluent"</note> celestial matter; it is obvious that air may be generated as often as terrestrial particles, small enough to be carried up and down by the celestial matter, ascend into the atmosphere. 

And if we prefer to think of the air as a collection <note>original: "congeries"</note> of little, slender springs, it seems not impossible (though it may be difficult) that the small parts of various bodies may, by a fortunate meeting of causes, be so connected as to constitute such little springs. Since (as we note in another treatise) water in the plants it nourishes is usually transformed <note>original: "contriv'd"</note> into springy bodies, even the mere altered position and connection of the parts of a body may be enough to give it a "spring" <note>elasticity</note> that it did not have before. This may be seen in a thin and flexible plate of silver, to which, by some strokes of a hammer, you may give a spring; yet by only heating it red-hot, you...

<summary>Boyle explains how, according to Cartesian physics, air could be formed by earthy or watery particles being suspended in "celestial matter," or by particles being mechanically reshaped into tiny "springs." He uses the example of hammered silver to show how physical manipulation can give a material elasticity (a "spring") it previously lacked.</summary>

...you may make it flexible again as before.

These, My Lord <note>Boyle is addressing his nephew, Charles Boyle, Lord Dungarvan, to whom the book is dedicated</note>, are some of the considerations occurring to my thoughts at present, by which it may be made probable that air may be generated anew. And although it is not impossible to propose objections against these, as well as against what has been represented in favor of the contrary doctrine; yet having already almost tired myself—and I fear more than almost tired Your Lordship—with such a troublesome inquiry into the nature of bubbles, I shall willingly leave Your Lordship to judge the arguments alleged on either side. 

I would scarcely have ventured to entertain you so long concerning such "empty things" <note>Boyle is making a pun here, referring both to the physical air inside a bubble and the perceived triviality of the topic</note> as bubbles, which have occasioned all this discourse, if I were not willing to invite you to notice with me the obscurity of things, or the dimness of our created intellects <note>human minds</note>. Of late, too many presume so much upon their own intellects that they either deny or censure the Almighty and omniscient Creator Himself. I hope we may learn from this the lesson that there are very many things in nature that we disdainfully overlook as obvious or despicable <note>original: "despicable" (split by the page break as "despi-" and catchword "cable")</note>...

<summary>Boyle concludes his argument that air might be newly generated from other substances and reflects on how even a "trivial" subject like bubbles reveals the limits of human understanding and the vastness of God's creation.</summary>

<margin>Experiment 23.</margin>
Since writing the twenty-first and twenty-second experiments (and despite everything delivered on those occasions concerning bubbles), we conducted some further trials to continue the same inquiry for which they were intended.

<margin>*Experiment 24*</margin>
After having thus discovered what effect the suction <note>original: "exsuction"</note> of the surrounding <note>original: "ambient"</note> air had upon water, we thought it good to also test what changes would occur in other liquids upon a similar removal of the pressure of the external air. We then took a glass egg-shaped flask <note>original: "Glass Egg"</note>, somewhat larger than a turkey egg, which had a long neck or stem about a third of an inch in diameter. Filling it with salad oil <note>original: "Sallet Oyl"</note> until it reached more than halfway to the top of the neck, we enclosed it in the vacuum chamber <note>original: "Receiver"</note> along with common water in a similar vessel, so that we might better compare the effect of the suction of the air upon those two liquids. When the pump was set to work, bubbles began to appear in the oil much sooner than in the water, and afterward, they also ascended much more abundantly <note>original: "copiously"</note> in the former liquid than in the latter. Indeed, after we had completely tired the person working the pump <note>original: "Pumper"</note> and almost exhausted our own patience, we

It was hard to imagine how this *phenomenon* could have resulted from any mistake in conducting the experiment; yet its strangeness prompted me to repeat it with fresh spirit of wine <note>original: "Spirit of Wine"; highly concentrated alcohol</note>. From this, it appears that the fundamental rule of **hydrostatics** <note>original: "Hydrostaticks"; the study of fluids at rest and the pressure they exert</note>—which states that an object will float in water if it is lighter than the volume of water it displaces—remains true even when atmospheric pressure is significantly reduced, or perhaps even when it is completely removed from both the liquid and the object. 

However, it is worth investigating what substance contributes so abundantly to filling the bubbles created in our experiment by the highly expanded air. To suggest, as the old **Aristotelian schools** <note>original: "Peripatetick Schools"; followers of Aristotle who believed air could expand to fill space without adding matter</note> did, that air undergoing **expansion** <note>original: "Rarefaction"</note> can occupy a larger space without the addition of any new substance, would be an explanation of this **phenomenon** <note>original: "Phenomenon"</note> that is very much out of date. I suspect our **modern scientists** <note>original: "Modern Naturalists"</note> would neither offer such an explanation nor accept it.

<margin>
Experiment 26.
</margin>
It has been observed by those who have studied **pendulums** (a subject of inquiry that may, in my humble opinion, be highly useful to **scientists** <note>original: "Naturalists"; those who study the laws of nature</note>) that their vibrations occur more slowly and their motion lasts for a shorter time in a thicker than in a thinner **medium** <note>the substance, such as air or water, through which an object moves</note>. We thought it worthwhile to test whether a **pendulum** would swing faster or continue swinging longer in our **receiver** <note>the glass vacuum chamber</note> after the removal <note>original: "exsuction"; the act of sucking out</note> of the air, compared to when air was present. Therefore, we took a pair of round and polished **pendulums** <note>the weights, or "bobs," of the pendulums</note> made of **iron** or **steel**. They were of equal size, as close as we could get the craftsman to make them, each weighing twenty **drams** <note>original: "Dragmes"; an apothecary weight equal to 1/8 of an ounce or roughly 3.9 grams</note> minus twenty **grains** <note>a very small unit of weight, roughly 65 milligrams</note>. One of these we suspended inside the **receiver** by a very thin silk string, about seven and a half inches long.

<margin>*Experiment 27.*</margin>

That the air is the medium through which sounds are conveyed to the ear has been for many ages, and is still, the common teaching <note>original: "Doctrine"</note> of the schools. But this accepted opinion has lately been opposed by some philosophers because of an experiment made by the industrious *Kircher* <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), a German Jesuit scholar who published influential works on acoustics</note> and other learned men. They have (as they assure us) observed that if a bell with a steel clapper is fastened to the inside of a tube, so that when conducting the experiment *concerning the vacuum* <note>original: "De Vacuo"</note> with that tube, the bell remains suspended in the empty space at the upper end of the tube. And if a strong magnet <note>original: "vigorous Load-stone"</note> is applied to the outside of the tube near the bell, it will attract the clapper; when the magnet is removed, the clapper falls back and strikes against the opposite side of the bell, thereby producing a very audible sound. From this, several people have concluded that it is not the air, but some more subtle substance <note>original: "Body"</note> that is the medium of sound.

But we believed that, in order to challenge <note>original: "invalidate"</note> such a conclusion from this clever experiment (though it is a most illuminating <note>original: "luciferous"; a term used by Francis Bacon to describe experiments that shed light on the nature of things</note>...

By the way, it is very well worth noting that in a vessel as well-sealed as our receiver, such a weak pulse as that of the balance wheel of a watch should transmit <note>original: "propagate"</note> a motion to the ear in a physically straight line, despite the presence of such a solid body as glass—especially glass as thick as that of our receiver. Other things invited us to consider whether, in the above-mentioned experiment made with the bell and the loadstone <note>original: "Load-stone"; a naturally magnetized piece of the mineral magnetite, used here to trigger the bell mechanism from outside the glass.</note>, there might not remain enough air in the evacuated <note>original: "deserted"</note> part of the tube to produce a sound. Since the tubes for the experiment concerning the vacuum <note>original: *De Vacuo*</note> (not to mention the usual thinness of the glass) are seldom made larger than is necessary, a little air might represent a significant <note>original: "not inconsiderable"</note> proportion of the evacuated space. 

Furthermore, in the experiment concerning the vacuum <note>original: *De Vacuo*</note>, as it is usually performed, there is generally some small amount of air that enters from outside, or at least a quantity <note>original: "store"</note> of bubbles that rise from the body of the mercury <note>original: "Quick-silver"</note> or other liquid itself.
